## Built by Phase 151e, lines 4 and 5 — a check and its result on one database, and the empty identity panel, 27 September 2026

- **The fault.** The test website builds the application on a database in memory (`api/index.mjs`), and each copy of
  the function has its own: every cold start, and any second copy Vercel runs beside the first. A result that reaches a
  copy which did not send the check finds nothing, and is refused. **It is still refused.** It is never matched to the
  nearest check.
- **What Vercel offers a function (read in its documentation on 27 September 2026, not from memory).** A read-only disk
  with a writable `/tmp` of up to 500 MB that is scratch space, not storage; Blob (files); Global Config (settings);
  and databases from its **Marketplace** (Neon, Supabase, Prisma Postgres and Nile for Postgres; Upstash and Redis;
  MongoDB; **Turso** for SQLite), each an account with its provider, whose address and token Vercel puts into the
  project's settings.
- **Why Turso.** Every call in this back end to `db.run`, `db.get` and `db.all` gets its answer at once. The `libsql`
  package (Turso's) answers the same way, in the same SQL, so `src/db.ts` changed and nothing above it did. A Postgres
  driver answers later and would change every one of those calls, and the SQL of 56 migrations. **Proved:** the whole
  back-end suite was run once with every database opened through `libsql` (1618 of 1620; the two that failed need the
  `.bak` files and git, which the copy it ran on did not have, and fail the same way on `node:sqlite` there). Two
  differences were found and are handled in `src/db.ts` (`asNodeSqliteRow`): libsql adds a `_metadata` field to each
  row, and gives bytes as an `ArrayBuffer`.
- **`src/db.ts`.** A database knows where it is: `db.kind` is `memory`, `file` or `remote` (`libsql://` or `https://`;
  plain `http://` is refused, and a remote database without its token is refused). `db.engine` says which library
  answers. The token is passed in by the caller (`createApp(location, { dbAuthToken })`), never read in `db.ts`.
- **`api/index.mjs` — SWITCHED OFF.** It uses the lasting database only when its address and token (`CAPREBATE_DB_URL`
  and `CAPREBATE_DB_TOKEN`, or the `TURSO_DATABASE_URL` and `TURSO_AUTH_TOKEN` Vercel's Turso integration adds)
  **and** `CAPREBATE_TEST_PASSWORD` are all set in the Vercel project's settings: a database that outlives the copy
  must never sit behind the seed password. It seeds only an empty database (`seedOnce`), and a copy that could not
  start tries again on the next request. The function log says which it chose, never the address or the token.
- **Saying so, whatever the database.** On a database in memory the identity panel shows **Records kept in memory**
  (`FORGETFUL_DATABASE_WORDS`), and a refused result that finds no check is audited with `database: "in memory"` and
  what to do, and answered `refused. <the same words>` (`FORGOTTEN_CHECK_WORDS`). The words come from `db.kind`: a page
  or a request cannot set them.
- **The empty panel.** A client with no contacts showed no form and said nothing. It now says **Nobody to check yet**,
  that a check goes to a contact of this client, and links to the **Add a contact** form (`NO_CONTACTS_WORDS`). The
  guide's B53 and the Help page say the same.
- **What is still true on a lasting database, for the developer:** uploaded documents go to the function's temporary
  disk (`documentRoot` in `api/index.mjs`), so a document uploaded through one copy is not there for another; the
  audit log's next number is read and then written, so two copies writing at the same instant can collide on it and
  one request fails (it cannot fork the chain: `seq` is the key); and `configureTestDeploy` sets every login to the test
  password at every cold start. **All three are dealt with since:** the documents and the audit log by Phase 151i (its
  section below, built and switched off), and the passwords by Phase 151j.
- Tests: `test/phase151e3.test.ts`, run `cd 09-app/backend && node --test test/phase151e3.test.ts` to count them.

## Built by Phase 151e, line 6 — contact details are required, 28 September 2026

Lakhbir, 28 September 2026: "we must get contact details it is a non negotiable", and, asked which rule, **both**.

- **No client without a way to reach them.** The website's funnel close (`applyClose` in `src/web/router.ts`) refuses a
  blank email address or telephone number, or one that does not fit the channel chosen (`contactValueProblem`: an email
  for email; 10 to 15 digits for a call or a text), in words, and saves nothing. What the person typed is now kept on
  their own contact as well as on the organisation, so every website client has a contact we can reach. The box is
  marked `required`. The site file's own funnel (`FN.submitContact`) says the same words before moving on; the
  gated-exit renderer proof's K1 to K4 check it.
- **Flagged until fixed.** A client with no contact that has an email address or a telephone number shows **Required**
  under **Contacts**, with a WE NEED line, until one is added (`reachableContacts` in `src/web/crm.ts`).
- **Never undone.** `updateContact` refuses the change that would clear the last email address and telephone number
  a client has (`LAST_WAY_TO_REACH_WORDS`). A contact added by staff may still lack both: the rule is about the client.
- **An identity check goes only to someone we can reach.** `sendIdentityCheck` refuses a contact with neither, before
  anything is sent; the panel offers only reachable contacts in **Who to check** and names the others, with a link.
- **Not enforced by the database.** The organisation row is written before its contact in the same transaction, and
  SQLite cannot check a rule across the two at the end of it. The two creation paths (the funnel close and the seed)
  are the only writers of `businesses`; a new one must call the same rule.
- **The seed** gave five of its six invented clients no contact, so they showed the flag; since Phase 151e line 7
  (below) each has an invented one.
- Tests: `test/phase151e4.test.ts`, run `cd 09-app/backend && node --test test/phase151e4.test.ts` to count them.

## Built by Phase 151e, line 7 — an invented contact on every seeded client; Turso, set up with Lakhbir, 28 September 2026

- **The seed** (`seedAContactForEveryClient`, last in `seed`): every client that has no contact gets one invented
  "Main Contact" — a role mailbox `contact@<key>.example.invalid` and a number in Ofcom's drama range. Dunmarrow keeps
  its two.
- **The test website never goes down for its database** (`openLasting` in `api/index.mjs`). With Turso set it opens
  it; if it cannot be opened, or holds a seed a first start never finished (the view-only login is written last, so
  users without it means a cut-off seed), it runs on memory and the function log says why — an error's code or name,
  never the address or the token. A copy that meets another copy seeding the same empty database waits up to ten
  seconds for it. Nothing ever tries to finish half a seed.
- **The first start** over the network makes about 60 statements and 57 schema steps (the 56 migrations and their record), then about 380 seed statements, one
  round trip each. **Put the Turso database in the region nearest the function** — Vercel's default is Washington,
  D.C. (`iad1`), so AWS `us-east-1` — or the first start can pass the function's time limit and leave a half seed (which the
  fallback above then reports: empty the database in Turso and redeploy). **It did, on 28 September 2026,** and worse: the
  limit was `vercel.json`'s own 30 seconds, and it stopped the first start part way through the MIGRATIONS, leaving
  `claim_facts` made and unrecorded, so every later start failed on "table claim_facts already exists". `maxDuration` is
  now 300 seconds, the most (and the default) Vercel's Hobby plan allows (read 28 Sep 2026), and the log line names this
  case and says to empty the database in Turso and redeploy (`HALF_BUILT_WORDS`). **The second database broke the same
  way for a different reason:** copies started in PAIRS within a second (11:46:09.38 and .70; 11:50:02 and :06), both
  migrated and seeded the one empty database, one hit "duplicate column name: legal_name", and the seed was left part
  done. **So the database is now built ONCE, while Vercel builds the deploy:** `vercel.json`'s `buildCommand` runs
  `09-app/backend/tools/prepare-lasting.mjs` (outside `api/`, where every file is a public address), which calls
  `prepareLasting` in `api/index.mjs` — the migrations and the seed by one process, before any copy starts. It always
  exits 0: a deploy never fails for the database, and a copy that finds it unfinished runs on memory. **Follow-up 5 (the
  same day; Lakhbir chose it):** the second database was half seeded as well, so now **a copy never builds or seeds the
  lasting database** — it opens it with `schema: 'require-built'` (`src/app.ts`; `pendingMigrations` in `src/db.ts`),
  refuses one with a table step missing (`E_NOT_BUILT`) or a seed not finished, writes nothing to it, and runs on memory
  saying to redeploy. **The build step empties a HALF-BUILT database and builds it again, once** (`emptyLasting`,
  `emptyHalfBuilt`: every view and table dropped in one batch with foreign keys off). No copy has ever run on a
  half-built database, so nothing anybody typed is in it; **a finished database (its view-only login written) is never
  emptied**, and a failure on one is reported and left alone. A seed that fails part way now says why. **Still true:** a
  migration and its record are not written as one step.
- **Lakhbir opens the account** (it is his: an account, and Turso's terms). Turso's pricing, read 28 September 2026:
  a free plan at $0, no card, 100 databases, 5 GB, 500 million rows read and 10 million written a month.
- Tests: `test/phase151e5.test.ts`, run `cd 09-app/backend && node --test test/phase151e5.test.ts` to count them.

## Built by Phase 151j — sign-in: forgot password, temporary passwords, a second step, 28 September 2026

Lakhbir, 28 September 2026: a forgotten password is reset by an emailed link; the super user creates an account with a
temporary password, and at the first sign-in the person chooses their own and sets up a second step; the second step
is **a code by email or an authenticator app** (text messages were decided against, so there is no SMS supplier);
**every staff login** must use one, **a client may**; and the super user assigns the role (already built: the role
decides what each person can see, on the server, in `can()`).

- **Enforced where a session is made.** `login()` (`src/auth.ts`) now checks the password with `verifyPassword`, asks
  `nextSignInStep` what is left — a temporary password to replace (`NEW_PASSWORD`), a second step to set up (`SETUP`)
  or to give (`VERIFY`) — and refuses with `E_SECOND_STEP` while anything is. So `/api/login` cannot go round it: it
  answered 401 "Sign in on the website…" until Phase 151k, which gave it the same steps (below). Only the website walked the steps (`src/web/sign-in.ts`), each a short-lived
  row in `sign_in_steps` (migration 057) held by an httpOnly cookie `cb_step`, and only a finished step issues a
  session (`startSession`).
- **The second step.** An authenticator app (RFC 6238, `src/totp.ts`, proved on the RFC's own test vectors: a QR code
  drawn as SVG by `uqr`, and the key as text; a code once only) or a six-digit code emailed to the person's own sign-in
  address (kept as a hash, ten minutes). Five wrong codes end the sign-in. A staff member can change theirs but not turn
  it off; a client can turn theirs on and off, at `/portal/two-factor` (staff: `/staff/two-factor`), from the user menu.
- **Temporary passwords.** The users page no longer takes a typed password: it makes one (16 characters with no
  look-alikes), shows it once, and it works for 7 days (`users.password_temporary_until`). Each row also has **New
  temporary password** and **Reset second step**; each ends that person's sessions. A temporary password past its date
  is refused with its own words.
- **A forgotten password.** `/login/forgot` and `/login/reset`, on the reset tokens migration 001 already had: the same
  answer for any address, a link good once for 30 minutes, the second step still asked afterwards, and a temporary
  password replaced by it.
- **A switch, and who may use it.** `createApp(…, { twoFactor: 'off' })` turns the steps off. The older test files and
  `tools/payload-proof.ts` pass it, each saying so; `test/phase151j.test.ts` runs on the default. Nothing in `src/` or
  `api/` passes it.
- **The test website** asks each seeded staff login to set up a second step at its first sign-in. **Emails are not sent
  there** (they go to the function log), so use the authenticator app there. `configureTestDeploy` now moves only the
  logins still on the public seed password, so a password a person chose survives a restart on the lasting database. *(Out of date since 30 September 2026: email is sent on the test website, and since Phase 154a1 the function log prints only how many emails, of which kinds, and how many were sent — never an address, a subject or a body.)*

**For the developer, as Phase 151j left it — and Phase 151k built all four, below:** the JSON API could not complete a
second step; the authenticator secret was stored as text; an emailed code or reset link stayed in the outbox body; and
the screenshot tool stopped at the step page. **Recovery codes** were not built here: a person who lost their phone
asked the super user to reset their second step. Lakhbir said **yes** to recovery codes on 28 September 2026 (Word
question 232, closed), and **Phase 151m built them** (below).

- Tests: `test/phase151j.test.ts`, run `cd 09-app/backend && node --test test/phase151j.test.ts` to count them.

## Built by Phase 151k — sign-in hardening: the API's steps, the app secret sealed, codes out of the outbox, the screenshot tool, 28 September 2026

The four things Phase 151j left, none waiting on anybody:

- **The JSON API walks the steps.** `/api/login` answers **202** with the step still to do and a `step_token`, never a
  session, and `/api/login/step/…` (`new-password`, `method`, `code`, `resend`; the bare path says where the step
  stands) finish it — a session, **200**, only when the last step is done. They call **the same functions** as the
  website's steps in `src/web/sign-in.ts`, on the same `sign_in_steps` rows, so the limits are the same: 15 minutes, 5
  wrong codes, a code once. A step begun on the API can be finished on the website, and a test does exactly that. The
  token travels in the JSON body, never in the address. `login()` itself still refuses with `E_SECOND_STEP`: only
  `src/web/sign-in.ts` walks steps. The website's own steps are unchanged.
- **The app's secret, sealed at rest** (`src/mfa-seal.ts`). **`CAPREBATE_MFA_KEY`** — 32 random bytes as base64,
  in the key file outside the repository (`~/.caprebate-secrets.env`, read by `src/secrets.ts`, which refuses a key
  file inside it), or on the test website in its own settings (`TEST_DEPLOY_KEYS` in `api/index.mjs`). With it,
  `users.mfa_secret` and `sign_in_steps.pending_secret` are AES-256-GCM (`cbmfa1:` then the nonce, tag and sealed
  bytes, with the person's id bound in, so a sealed secret copied onto another login does not open). **With no key it
  stays as today:** the secret is kept as text. A text secret from before still works, and is sealed the next time it
  signs its person in, or all at once by `node src/cli.ts seal-app-secrets`. **A sealed secret without its key — or
  with another — refuses in words** ("We cannot check app codes on this server just now…") and is not counted as a
  wrong code; **a key set but not 32 bytes** refuses to set up an app, in words, and email codes still work. **Keep a
  copy of the key:** lose it and every app user must have their second step reset by the super user.
- **Codes and links out of the outbox.** A sign-in code is now an email of its own kind, `SIGN_IN_CODE`. Once an email
  of that kind or `PASSWORD_RESET` has **really been sent** (`dispatchOutbox`, which runs only while `EMAIL_SENDING` is
  on), its body becomes one fixed sentence — *"This email held a sign-in code or a password reset link. It was sent,
  and then its words were removed so the code or link is not kept."* — and who it went to, when, the subject and the
  kind stay. Migration 058 lets that one edit through the outbox's never-edited rule and no other. **While sending is
  off nothing is taken out**: an unsent row is the only copy, and on the test website the function log prints it
  (`api/index.mjs`) — which is how a person there reads a code or a reset link. So until sending is on, those bodies
  stay in the outbox, and anyone who can read the database can read them for as long as they are valid (a code 10
  minutes, a link 30). *(Phase 154a1, 4 October 2026: the function log no longer prints an outbox row at all — counts and kinds only — and sending is on for testing.)*
- **The screenshot tool** (`04-phase-work/phase144/tools/crawl-shots.mjs`) signs in through
  `tools/demo-sign-in.ts` and the JSON API's steps: on a demo just started it sets up an authenticator app for each
  staff login and makes the codes itself; an emailed code it reads from the demo's console (`npm run start:demo >
  demo.log`, then `CB_DEMO_LOG=demo.log`). It refuses any login not `@example.invalid` and any website not on this
  machine, so it never touches the test website's logins. **What it can no longer reach:** a login whose app was set up
  before the run (it never held the key) and a login with a temporary password to replace — it says which, and why, as
  "NOT REACHED", and a fresh demo reaches them all. Run on 28 September 2026: before, the staff logins reached only the
  sign-in page; after, all six roles reached their screens.
- Tests: `test/phase151k.test.ts`, run `cd 09-app/backend && node --test test/phase151k.test.ts` to count them. Proved
  red on the code as it was before this phase.

## Built by Phase 151m — recovery codes: ten one-use codes, so a person who loses their phone can still sign in, 28 September 2026

Lakhbir, 28 September 2026 (Word question 232, closed): yes to recovery codes. Until now a person who lost their phone
had to ask the super user to reset their second step.

- **The codes** (`src/web/recovery-codes.ts`). Ten at a time, each ten characters from a set of 32 with no look-alikes
  (A to Z without I and O, and 2 to 9; one case, so a code can be read out and typed either way), in two groups of five:
  **50 bits each**. Kept **only as a hash** in `recovery_codes` (migration 059: user, hash, made, used) — scrypt
  (N 16384, r 8, p 1, about 30 ms a try) salted with the person's own id, so one hash is worked out per try and compared
  in constant time with each unused code, and a copy of the table is no quicker to try than the sign-in page. Never kept
  as text, never in a log line or an audit row, never emailed. A trigger keeps a used code used and its row unchanged.
- **When they are made, and shown once.** When a second step is set up — staff and clients alike, at the first sign-in
  or from **Your second step** — the answer to that step **is the recovery codes page** (200, the session already
  issued; its one link, *I have saved them. Continue*, goes where the redirect used to). It says: save or print them,
  each works once, they will not be shown again. **Make new recovery codes** on `/staff/two-factor` and
  `/portal/two-factor` makes ten more and throws every unused old one away. The JSON API gets the same on **the same
  functions** (`submitCode`, `makeNewRecoveryCodes` in `src/web/sign-in.ts`): the setting-up answer carries
  `recovery_codes` once, and `POST /api/account/recovery-codes` makes new ones. Audited `RECOVERY_CODES_MADE` with why,
  how many were made and how many unused ones were thrown away.
- **Using one** (`submitRecoveryCode`). At the VERIFY step only, on the website (*Use a recovery code instead*,
  `/login/step?use=recovery`, posting to `/login/step/recovery`) and on the API (**its own route**,
  `/api/login/step/recovery`, not `/code`). It **counts toward the same 5 wrong codes**, works once (the row is taken only
  while unused, so two sign-ins racing on one code cannot both win), is audited `RECOVERY_CODE_USED` with how many are
  left and never the code, and emails the person that one was used and how many remain — a new outbox kind,
  `RECOVERY_CODE_USED`, which carries **no code**, so it is **not** one of the kinds `blankSentSecrets` empties. The
  session's audit row says `second_factor: RECOVERY_CODE`. **With 2 or fewer left**, the page after sign-in is **Your
  second step**, which says so in red and offers new ones, and the email says to make new ones soon. A person with a
  second step set up before this phase has none, so their next sign-in lands there too, telling them to make some.
- **What clears them.** Setting up a new second step (new ones are made at once), the super user's **Reset second step**
  and a client turning theirs off all delete the person's codes; the `TWO_FACTOR_REMOVED` row counts them. **A new
  temporary password does not.**
- **Words.** The step page's line now names recovery codes first (*Lost your phone? Use one of your recovery codes. No
  codes left? Then ask your administrator…*; a client is told to contact us); the guide's Admin section and `B55`
  (and so Help) say what they are, where to keep them and what to do when they run out, at grade 7.
- **The test website** applies migration 059 when it builds (`tools/prepare-lasting.mjs`), as it did 058.
- **For the developer:** printing is the browser's own Print (no script: the pages carry no inline script under the
  Content-Security-Policy). There is no download of the codes as a file.
- Tests: `test/phase151m.test.ts`, run `cd 09-app/backend && node --test test/phase151m.test.ts` to count them. Proved
  red on the code as it was before this phase. `test/phase151j.test.ts` and `test/phase151k.test.ts` now expect the
  recovery codes page, not a redirect, after a second step is set up on the website.

## Built by Phase 151g — the test lookups Lakhbir allowed: the smoke test keeps his five conditions, 28 September 2026

Lakhbir, 25 September 2026 (Word question 170): yes to a few small, logged test lookups to check a data connector
works, on five conditions — no more than three lookups per connector each time it is switched on; never a client's
property; keep only pass or fail, the time and the connector; log who ran it and why; never use it to show the product
to clients. Until this phase the smoke command printed a warning and enforced none of them.

- **Where.** `src/connectors/test-lookups.ts` (`runSmokeCommand`, `runSwitchedOnCommand`); `src/cli.ts` hands it the
  live transport and the key file. `src/connectors/smoke.ts` still makes the one call and is unchanged in behaviour.
- **The log.** `CAPREBATE_TEST_LOOKUP_LOG`, default `~/.caprebate-test-lookups.jsonl`, one JSON line each: a
  `switched_on` line (connector, time, who, why) or a `lookup` line (connector, time, `PASS` or `NO`, who, why, and
  `not_a_client: true` where that flag was given). Refused inside the repository, as `src/secrets.ts` refuses a key
  file. Created with mode 600. A line it did not write refuses the next lookup; nothing edits or rotates the file.
- **The one page** at §3.2 carries the command and a table of the five conditions and what enforces each.
- **Tests:** `test/phase151g.test.ts` — each condition refused and each allowed, on an invented log in a temporary folder
  with an empty key file, so no call can leave; one PASS in-process on a recorded answer. Every test was red on the
  pre-change `src/cli.ts` and `src/connectors/smoke.ts`. Run `cd 09-app/backend && node --test test/phase151g.test.ts`
  to count them.

**For the developer, still true:** the log is on the machine that runs the command; a second machine keeps its own
count. Two people running the command at the same moment could both read "two used" and both run — it is a person's
command, not a service, and the log shows it afterwards. The count starts on a person's word (`smoke-switched-on`),
not on the switch in `REGISTER_SWITCHES` itself, because a smoke test runs while that switch is still off.

## Built by Phase 151l — the land boundary's first download, as a test lookup under the five conditions, 28 September 2026

The land boundary fetcher (Phase 150) is built and switched off, and HM Land Registry does not publish the element
names inside its GML, so the first allowed download must confirm the shape. Phase 151g built Lakhbir's five conditions
into the smoke command, which did not cover a download. This phase makes the download a test lookup.

- **Which, and why.** HMLR_INSPIRE is **a source the smoke command accepts** (`smoke HMLR_INSPIRE`, and
  `smoke-switched-on HMLR_INSPIRE`), not a command of its own — so the notice, `CAPREBATE_LIVE_SMOKE=1`, `--who` and
  `--why`, the log, the count of three, the never-a-client check and the line written PASS or NO are **the same code**
  in `runSmokeCommand`, not a copy. Only the values (a point and a council) and the call differ, in
  `src/connectors/boundary-test-lookup.ts`. `TEST_LOOKUP_SOURCES` is the one list both commands accept.
- **Public values.** The default point is 70 Whitehall's (the Treasury Buildings, the Cabinet Office), from Historic
  England's National Heritage List for England, entry 1267063: TQ 30120 79956, so easting 530120 and northing 179956;
  its council is the City of Westminster (`E09000033`, file `City_of_Westminster`). Only the council's name leaves.
- **Never a client.** With `CAPREBATE_DB`: a point within `CLIENT_POINT_RADIUS_M` (250 metres) of a point a person
  entered or Homedata gave for a property is refused, and so is a council a person added for one — a cautious
  distance, because a site's size is not known before its boundary is. Without it: anything but the defaults needs
  `--not-a-client`, and the line says so. The same `clientCheck` does both connectors.
- **A PASS** is the file downloaded, every polygon read by the existing reader, and the polygon holding the point found.
  The fetcher is handed no file store and its answer is dropped: nothing kept, nothing stored, nothing printed but a
  stage and a sentence. `BOUNDARY_FETCHING_SWITCHED_ON` is not imported: the fetcher is switched on for its one call,
  as `smokeOne` switches one register on. The audit row, in a database in memory, reports both gates shut.
- **Tests:** `test/phase151l.test.ts` — each condition refused and allowed for the boundary, a PASS, a NO on a file the
  reader refuses, a NO on a file with no polygon at the point, a NO on a 404, and the shipped constant untouched, on
  the invented phase150 zips and an invented log in a temporary folder, with no network. Every test was red on the
  pre-change `src/connectors/test-lookups.ts`. `test/phase150.test.ts`'s guard now allows exactly this file beside the
  site area function, with the switch-on in one place and the smoke command the only way in.

**Still to do, and whose:** running it. The download is Lakhbir's to run, or a person he names, after he records the
switch-on (`smoke-switched-on HMLR_INSPIRE`). A PASS confirms the shape; switching the fetcher on for the application
is still his, separately. If the Treasury Buildings are Crown land with no registered freehold, the default gives NO
at "not found" — which still proves the shape, because every polygon was read first, and the output says so.

## Built by Phase 151i — uploaded documents on a store every copy reaches, and the audit log on a shared database, 28 September 2026

With the lasting database on (Phase 151e), a document uploaded through one copy of the test website was still written
to that copy's temporary disk, missing from the next copy while its row said it was there. And two copies appending to
the audit log at once could collide on its next number, and lose a request.

- **What Vercel offers (read in its documentation on 28 September 2026, not from memory).** Vercel Blob, with **private
  stores**: every read and write needs a token; an object's address (`https://<store>.private.blob.vercel-storage.com/…`)
  "is not publicly accessible"; the `@vercel/blob` SDK (2.3 or later for private; 2.8.0 is installed) signs in by the
  project's OIDC token with `BLOB_STORE_ID`, or by `BLOB_READ_WRITE_TOKEN`. `get` gives null for a missing object,
  `head` throws `BlobNotFoundError`, `del` of a missing object is not an error, and a deleted object "may take up to
  one minute" to leave Blob's cache.
- **The port did not change.** `ObjectStore` (the upload handler's) already answers with promises. Callers also use four
  things of `FsObjectStore` itself (the upload link check, the client-bytes write, opening a kept original, and the
  kept-days setting), so the new store **is** an `FsObjectStore`: `SharedObjectStore` in `src/web/shared-objectstore.ts`
  replaces only the four steps that say where bytes go (`readBytes`, `writeBytes`, `copyBytes`, `removeProven`, added
  to `src/web/objectstore.ts`, where the folder's behaviour is unchanged). Every rule stays in the base class: the key
  checked, an original sealed before it is kept and refused with no key, a deletion proved, a link scoped to one key.
- **Private.** Every write says `access: 'private'`, `application/octet-stream`, no random suffix, and Blob's shortest
  cache (60 seconds); every read skips the cache. A store created public by mistake refuses the write, so it fails
  closed. The address Blob gives back is thrown away. A document leaves only through the routes that already authorise
  each request. The upload link is still ours, `/upload/put?token=…`, for one key.
- **One upload link secret.** A link issued by one copy is used on whichever copy Vercel picks, so the shared store signs
  links with a secret derived (HKDF-SHA256) from the lasting database's token, which every copy holds. With a secret made
  fresh in each process, as the folder store does, the second copy refuses a good link.
- **SWITCHED OFF.** `api/index.mjs` (`sharedDocumentStore`, `sharedDocumentOptions`, `documentsWhy`) uses it only when
  the lasting database is **in use** (it opened; a copy that fell back to memory keeps the folder) **and** a Blob
  setting is present: `BLOB_STORE_ID`, or a read-write token (`CAPREBATE_BLOB_TOKEN`, else `BLOB_READ_WRITE_TOKEN`).
  `openLasting` hands the store to the lasting database only, never to memory. `@vercel/blob` is loaded only then. The
  function log says which it chose, never a token or the store's id. The one page at §3.2 carries the row.
- **The audit log.** `AuditLog.append` read the last hash and then, in a second statement, the next number. Now one
  statement reads both from the same row, and a clash on `seq` (another copy wrote first) is read again and retried, up
  to `APPEND_ATTEMPTS`. `Db.tx` begins `IMMEDIATE`, taking the write lock first, so a transaction that reads the chain
  and writes to it cannot meet another copy's row in between; a second handle on a file waits `BUSY_TIMEOUT_MS` for it.
  What a hash covers is unchanged, and `npm run verify-audit` reads the chain as before.
- **Tests:** `test/phase151i.test.ts`, on a Blob store that answers as the SDK's documentation says (no network): a
  document, its retained text and a sealed original written by one copy and read by another; two copies of the website
  on one database file, Sales uploading through one and Admin opening the kept original through the other; nothing
  without a session or for the wrong role; the switch in every position; the audit log with two handles in turn,
  interleaved two ways, and across two processes, then `verify-audit`. Eleven were red on the pre-change
  `objectstore.ts`, `server.ts`, `audit.ts`, `db.ts` and `api/index.mjs` (the new file kept), each for its own reason
  (the chain forked; the number clashed and the request was lost; "database is locked"; the second copy answered 500;
  the bytes went to the copy's own disk); the other six guard what did not change (the folder store, appends in turn,
  `verify-audit`) or what lives only in the new file (the shared link secret, the token, the key check). Run `cd 09-app/backend && node --test test/phase151i.test.ts` to count them.

**Follow-up, the same day: why Turso would not open.** The live website's first real attempt logged "the lasting database
could not be opened ()": libsql's errors carry an EMPTY code and put everything in the message. `openFailure` in
`api/index.mjs` now prints the code (or name) and the library's own words, with the address, its host, the token and
anything shaped like a token or an address cut out, 200 characters at most. Two tests in `test/phase151i.test.ts`,
red on the pre-change entry point; `test/phase151e5.test.ts`'s pattern follows the new words.

**For the developer, still true:** a document deleted from the store can sit in Blob's own cache for up to a minute,
behind the same authentication; the upload link secret changes when the database token does (a link lasts fifteen
minutes); and each upload is three or four Blob writes, which counts against Vercel's free operations.

## Built by Phase 152 — the sweep's four builds: the confirmation link, migrations as one step, the cold start, a flaky test, 28 September 2026

- **The sign-up confirmation link leaves the outbox once sent.** `ACCOUNT_VERIFY` joins `SIGN_IN_CODE` and
  `PASSWORD_RESET` in `SENT_SECRET_KINDS` (`src/stubs/email-provider.ts`): its link works once and for 24 hours
  (`src/web/signup.ts`), and only its hash is kept in `client_signups`, so the link still works after its words go. The
  fixed sentence is now *"This email held a sign-in code or a one-use link. It was sent, and then its words were removed
  so the code or link is not kept."* Migration 060 re-creates `outbox_body_never_edited` for the three kinds and that
  sentence only; rows 058 blanked keep its sentence and are never edited again. While sending is off nothing is taken
  out, as before.
- **A migration and its record land together or not at all** (`migrate` and `splitMigration` in `src/db.ts`). Before,
  a file ran and THEN its `schema_migrations` row was written, so a start cut off between the two left the file's tables
  made and unrecorded, and every later start failed on them ("table claim_facts already exists" — the test website's
  first start on Turso, 28 September). Now a plain file and its record run in one transaction; a file that runs its own
  (`PRAGMA foreign_keys = OFF;`, a line `BEGIN;`, its body, a line `COMMIT;`, `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;` — 007, 011,
  014, 016 to 019, 049, 053, 054 and 056) is split at its own `BEGIN;` and `COMMIT;`: the pragma outside, the body AND
  the record in one transaction, the closing pragma always after. **Any other shape is refused with a sentence and
  nothing from it lands** — two `BEGIN;` lines, a `COMMIT;` with no `BEGIN;`, `BEGIN TRANSACTION`, or anything but the
  one pragma around a file's own transaction. A new migration that needs its own transaction must use that one shape.
- **The cold start, measured.** On the Mac, on memory, `build()` in `api/index.mjs` takes about 0.73 s: importing the
  server 0.34 s, the migrations and the site file 0.27 s, the seed 0.05 s, moving the logins to the test password 0.07
  s. A copy opening a finished lasting database makes 6 database calls, and 7 more for the test password. The two
  504s of 28 September came before Phase 151i follow-ups 3 to 5, when a copy still built Turso itself under a 30-second
  limit. On the live website the first request after an hour idle answered 200 in 4.8 s. Each copy now prints
  "test deployment: this copy started in N ms".
- **The grid tool's test no longer reads a slow machine as a wrong answer** (`test/phase151c.test.ts`). Each run of
  `tools/ostn15-verify.ts` reads the whole 876,951-point grid: about 2 s alone, 8.5 s beside 30 busy processes, and
  180 s in one full run on 25 September, killed at the old 120 s limit with status null — which read as a wrong
  answer. The limit is 15 minutes, and a run stopped at its limit now fails saying STOPPED, not as an exit code. A run
  that finishes is judged as before: the 2 mm case must still exit 1.
- **Tests:** `test/phase152.test.ts` — the confirmation link (five were red on the pre-change `email-provider.ts` with
  060 set aside; the sixth guards sending-off) and the migrations (the two cut-off tests were red on the pre-change
  `db.ts` with "table claim_facts already exists" and "table finance_settings already exists"; the shape test was red
  there too; a clean run is the control). The cut-off test kills a child process with SIGKILL right after a file's SQL
  has run, on a file database; it was also run by hand on the libsql engine, which rolled 015 back and applied 015 on.
  Run `cd 09-app/backend && node --test test/phase152.test.ts` to count them.

## 5.1 · The eight things this build honestly does not have

Each of these is outside what could be built here, and each is on the critical
path to a public deployment. **The CRM adds seven more of the same kind, at §5.5.**

**RE-DERIVED BY PHASE 146g, 23 SEPTEMBER 2026, now that the connectors are real and switched
off.** It is still eight, and the numbering does not move — items are discussed by number. **Two
of them were written when nothing called anything and had gone plainly false**: item 2 said
there were no secrets in this build at all "because nothing calls anything", and item 5 priced
the accounts as future work. Both are rewritten below. **Items 1, 3, 4, 6, 7 and 8 are
unchanged and were re-read, not assumed.** Everything a developer now needs about an account, a
key, a switch and a precondition is **the one page at the top of §3.2**, and §5.1 does not
restate it.

1. **TLS and certificates.** The application sets `Secure` on every cookie and
   assumes it is behind HTTPS. It terminates nothing itself. Certificate issue,
   renewal and HSTS are yours.

2. **Production secrets management — and the moment this item anticipated has
   arrived.** _Rewritten by Phase 146g, 23 September 2026. It used to read "there are no secrets
   in this build at all … because nothing calls anything", and it ended "the moment a connector
   exists that changes". The connectors exist._ **There are still no secrets in the repository
   and none in this document, and there never may be.** What exists now is the machinery for
   them: `src/secrets.ts` reads ONE file, **outside the code folder** — `~/.caprebate-secrets.env`
   or the path in `CAPREBATE_SECRETS` — once, at start-up; **the back end refuses a key file
   inside the code folder**; every key has a named owner and a connector may read only its own;
   the store prints as **names, never values**; error text passes `redactSecrets`, and the audit
   screen refuses a detail that carries a key. `09-app/backend/secrets.example.env` is the list
   of names, and a test holds it against `SECRET_KEYS`. **A vault (or the host's equivalent)
   replaces that file before the first key is issued for anything but a developer's laptop** —
   see part 2 of "What the build needs from a host" in §3.2.

3. **A web application firewall**, and rate limiting at the edge. There is
   application-level rate limiting on authentication and reset; there is nothing
   in front of the process.

4. **Penetration testing.** Not performed. The controls here are unit-tested and
   inspected on the wire, which is not the same thing and is not offered as the
   same thing.

5. **Real API credentials and their licences.** _Re-derived by Phase 146g, 23 September 2026:
   the connectors are built, so this is no longer a list of things to find out — it is a list of
   accounts to open, and **the one page at the top of §3.2 is that list**, with the key name, the
   switch, the precondition, the cost and the smoke test for every one._ What still belongs here
   is what money and time buy rather than code:
   - **Lead times**, and they are the reason to start early: **EPC** registration through GOV.UK One Login; and **HMRC Agent
     Services** with the 64-8 flow if it is ever wanted. (The **HMLR Business Gateway** account and its
     network-access agreement are **no longer needed**: since Phase 146k the title register is looked up by
     hand.)
   - **Licence flags that are a solicitor's question and not an engineering judgement**: (HMLR title
     redistribution to the client's accountant was answered YES by the solicitor on 27 September 2026,
     Phase 146m) the CQC syndication currency obligation (the cache
     window is held at **0 days** until it is answered), and whether a derived £/m² printed in a
     report counts as reproducing the BCIS table (and, since Phase 151 read BCIS's licence, whether our
     figure is an "extract relating to projects created in the service": held as NO until BCIS confirms).
   - **A mailbox is a precondition, not an afterthought.** contact@caprebate.co.uk must exist, with
     SPF, DKIM and DMARC, before planning or email can be switched on: PlanIt blocks a request
     whose User-Agent names no contact.
   - **A file the host must carry**: the ONS postcode table, `CAPREBATE_POSTCODE_DB`, about 75 MB,
     built by the ingest. It is a **precondition of the CQC lookup**, whose API cannot be searched
     by postcode. **And two more for the site area (Phase 151c)**: Ordnance Survey's OSTN15 grid,
     `CAPREBATE_OSTN15_FILE`, checked once with `tools/ostn15-verify.ts`, and a folder outside the code
     for the land boundary files, `CAPREBATE_INSPIRE_STORE_DIR`, swept daily with
     `node src/cli.ts sweep-land-boundaries`. Both are read where the research run starts, the server.
   - **Money, in one place: £50 to Homedata, not £20.** The first payment an organisation ever makes
     is matched 100%, once, ever. See §3.2.
   - **A measurement before a switch: CA7.** The 200-property matching measurement
     (`09-app/backend/tools/match-measure.ts`, built and refusing to run) sets the confidence
     thresholds, and it waits on the data protection officer. **Run it before any free lookup is
     switched on.**

6. **Email deliverability.** Stub 1 is a swap; deliverability is not. SPF, DKIM,
   DMARC, a sending domain and its reputation, and bounce handling.
   **Invoice fraud is the specific risk here**: bank details on an emailed
   invoice are the standard vector and the sums are percentages of tax refunds.
   Details are confirmed on a channel other than the invoice email and **never
   changed because an email asked**. That is procedural and belongs in the SOP —
   **do not build the invoice email flow before it is written down.** **CRM 3 of 10 holds
   this in code:** a template carrying an invoice or bank details is refused by its store, by
   its table and again at the merge, and no automation rule has an effect that writes to a
   client at all. **CRM 7 of 10 wrote the procedure down — into SOP 9, as a flagged DRAFT
   ADDITION, not in force** until the solicitor, the data protection officer and the developer
   sign it off. `INVOICE_FRAUD_PROCEDURE` in `src/web/automation.ts` says so, a test fails if
   the code says in force while the SOP says draft, and the same two refusals now also cover a
   reminder, bank details sent by a rule, payment details, where to pay, a bank transfer, an
   amount due, a fee note and a statement of account.

7. **The hosting itself.** UK data residency is a requirement. Nothing here has
   been deployed anywhere.

8. **The WordPress integration — and this is the decision that changes the
   price.** Everything built is TypeScript. **If the host can run a Node process,
   this ships behind WordPress and the integration is a reverse proxy and a
   shared session story. If it cannot, this becomes the executable specification
   a PHP rebuild is tested against** — the tests define *done* either way, so no
   work is thrown away, but the two are different quotes. See 5.3.

### 5.1a · The three files a production server needs — Phase 155 (line 6), 4 October 2026

**For whoever chooses the production host.** Three lookups read a large file from the server's own disk rather than
calling anyone. The test website runs on Vercel functions, which cannot hold them (a function's bundle is capped at a few
hundred megabytes and its disk lasts only as long as the copy), so **on the test website all three are absent, and the
code says so rather than pretending**: no default path, no copy in the repository or the deployment, no download on first
use. Each is named by a setting, must sit **outside the repository** (the code refuses a path inside it where it checks),
and with it absent everything still works and the fact it would have given stays asked (N4). Read 4 October 2026.

| | **The ONS postcode table** | **OSTN15, the coordinate grid** | **The land boundary store** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Setting** | `CAPREBATE_POSTCODE_DB` — a SQLite file | `CAPREBATE_OSTN15_FILE` — a text file | `CAPREBATE_INSPIRE_STORE_DIR` — a folder |
| **What it is for** | A postcode's council area and nation: the CQC lookup searches by council area and cannot search by postcode, and the energy certificate, sale price and planning lookups use the nation when nobody recorded it (`src/web/postcode.ts`, `src/connectors/postcode.ts`) | Turning a supplier's latitude and longitude into the British National Grid the land boundaries are drawn in, for the site area (`src/connectors/ostn15.ts`) | Each council's land boundary file, kept for its month so one download serves every property in that council (`src/connectors/inspire-store.ts`) |
| **Where it comes from** | The ONS Postcode Directory (or the National Statistics Postcode Lookup), ONS Open Geography Portal, **quarterly** (August 2026 is current). Our ingest builds the table from it | Ordnance Survey's free developer pack, `OSTN15-OSGM15-DevelopersPack.zip` (ordnancesurvey.co.uk), file `OSTN15_OSGM15_DataFile.txt`. Fixed: it does not change | HM Land Registry's INSPIRE Index Polygons, one `.zip` of GML per council (318), **republished on the first Sunday of each month**; downloaded on demand by `src/connectors/inspire.ts`, a council at a time |
| **Licence** | Open Government Licence v3.0, with three credits (ONS; "Contains OS data © Crown copyright and database right"; "Contains Royal Mail data © Royal Mail copyright and database right"). **Northern Ireland (BT) postcodes need a separate licence from Land and Property Services for commercial use** — the reader already refuses them | BSD (Open Source Initiative); software that includes it carries "© Copyright and database rights Ordnance Survey Limited 2016" | Open Government Licence, with HM Land Registry's and Ordnance Survey's two attribution statements (the source register's `HMLR_INSPIRE` row). Kept no longer than 35 days. Boundary maps stay out of client reports (Lakhbir, 15 September 2026) |
| **Size** | The download: about **254 MB** zipped, **1.5 GB** as CSV (ONSPD, August 2026). The table we build: **about 75 MB** | **15.5 MB** zipped; the data file is **41 MB** | All councils together: about **5 GB** zipped (about 33–35 GB unpacked, which we never do — the store keeps each `.zip` as downloaded). In practice only the councils our clients are in, a few to a few hundred MB each |
| **How it is put there** | Download the release, run the ingest, set the path. Repeat each quarter | Download once, check it with `tools/ostn15-verify.ts` (the pack's forty test points), set the path | Set the folder; it fills itself. `node src/cli.ts sweep-land-boundaries` once a day deletes what is past its days |
| **Without it** | No council area and no nation from the postcode: the CQC lookup is not made and says why | Every latitude and longitude is refused; no site area | Nothing is kept: each lookup downloads its council's file again — slower, never wrong |

**What the host therefore needs:** a persistent disk of about **1 GB** to start (the postcode table, the grid, and a few
councils' boundary files), growing with the councils clients are in; a scheduled job for the daily sweep and the
quarterly postcode refresh; and nothing of the three in the repository or the built image. **This is a deployment
decision, not a code change**: every path is a setting already. Phase 155 built nothing that pretends the files are on the
test website.

Checked against Homedata on 23 September 2026: **no equivalent exists** in its 40+ endpoints, and its `valuation_estimate` and `valuation_full` are **residential sale-price estimates that must never stand in for BCIS or the Valuation Office** — a modelled price standing where a fact belongs is an N4 breach, not a saving |

## 5.3 · The open questions — so nobody quotes a moving target

Quote these **by title**. _(Phase 146a, 22 September 2026: this said "the questions document does not number them". The Word questions document, Caprebate_Questions.docx, is the source of truth for questions and answers since 22 September 2026, and it numbers them; each title below is the monday.com one it was written under, with the Word number and title beside it.)_

| Question | Why it is on this page |
|---|---|
| **“Does WordPress replace the rest of the system, or sit beside it?”** (Word question 154, “Should WordPress replace our whole system, or sit beside it?”) | **It decides wrap versus rebuild, and therefore the price.** It was answered on 1 September with the larger reading — WordPress is the whole back end and front end — but **whether the host can run a Node process has never been answered**, and if it can, most of the rebuild disappears. **Get it confirmed in one sentence before pricing.** **Since CRM 10 of 10 the price carries the CRM's size — §5.5 item 6.** |
| **“Did you send the two hosting questions, or were ‘yes and yes’ the host's answers?”** (Word question 2, “Did you send the two hosting questions, or did the host say yes?”) | The same two questions — *can the client-document folders be excluded from the backup*, and *can the host run Node* — were sent to the host on 3 September and the reply reads equally as *I will send them* and as *the host said yes to both*. **A data-protection control that rests on a remembered conversation is not a control.** |
| **“Can the client-document folders be excluded from the nightly whole-site backup?”** (Word question 155, “Can the host leave our two client-document folders out of its backups?”) | **The only question on this list that can stop a build.** A file unlinked from disk and alive in a 30-day host backup **has not been deleted, and the deletion record would say it had.** If the answer is no, the client-document upload page cannot ship as designed. **Since Phase 132 the cycle is a named blank rather than a guess**: `BACKUP_CYCLE` in `04-phase-work/phase3/caprebate-client-doc-ingestion.ts` is NOT SET, owned by the data protection officer with the developer. `recordDeletion` writes it onto every deletion's evidence as backups still pending, with no cycle and no expiry. `ret3DueAt` stays null rather than treating the copies as expired, and `assertRestoreApproved` refuses a restoration without the data protection officer's approval |
| **“Which receipt strip should each new document type carry before anyone may upload one?”** (Word question 167, “What should be removed from each of 14 new document types before upload?”) | Fourteen document types — the engagement letter, accounts, the fee invoice, the survey and capital allowances reports and the rest — **are recorded, configurable and switched off**: nothing of them can be received until each has a receipt policy. The decision is the data protection officer's. **Since Phase 129 a recommended strip for each is recorded and switched off, with a one-line reason to approve or amend** — and binding one is a policy entry of its own in the ingestion module plus a migration that rebuilds the type table, not one line |
| **“May company names stay on conveyancing uploads if we strip the individuals?”** (Word question 160, “On property sale papers, may we keep company names but remove people's names?”) | The parties clause of a sale contract and the pre-contract enquiry replies is the fact entitlement turns on, and today none of it is captured. **The strip that keeps company names and numbers and removes every individual is built and switched off (Phase 129)**; the answer switches it on, or says those two document types cannot be processed at all |
| **“Who exactly can administer the website — and does that include the host's own staff?”** (Word question 157, “Exactly who can run the website, including the host's own staff?”) | The audit log is **tamper-evident, not tamper-proof**: administrators sit inside the trust boundary and are trusted. That determination carries three conditions and **one — that the administrator set is enumerated and known — is not met today** |
| **The engagement letter's blanks — eight of them** | Derived from the document at request time, not listed by hand. **Clause 1** the introduced-adviser fee · **clause 4** the fee basis, the payment terms and the abort fee · **clause 5** the break fee · **clause 13** the trigger, whether it reaches HMRC settlements, and the time limit. They sit under three question titles: *“What is the break fee, and is there ever an abort fee or a fixed fee?”*, *“If a claim is reduced after we have been paid, do we give the money back?”* and *“Do we cap the fee, warn about it, or adjust the percentage?”* **The prototype shows them blank, loudly. A plausible number in a prototype becomes a number in a contract.** |
| **“What replaces the paid address autocomplete in the funnel?”** (Word question 17, “What should clients type their address into instead of a paid lookup?”) and **“What does Homedata actually cost per lookup?”** (Word question 16, “How much does a Homedata address lookup cost each time?”) | A purchasing decision. **It does not block Block B** — a typed postcode and address field is a working fallback and is what the block should be priced against. **Decided 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c):** Lakhbir agreed a typed postcode and address; an autocomplete is bought only if the 200-property measurement shows typed addresses losing good enquiries |
| **“Will the data protection officer permit a transient read of two register name fields?”** (Word question 156, “May we briefly check two public register names without saving them?”) | Blocks DS-W9 and nothing else |
| **“What does ‘24 working hours’ mean?”** (Word question 12, “What does our promise to call back within ‘24 working hours’ mean?”) and **“What is the contact telephone number?”** (Word question 13, “What phone number should go on the thank-you screen?”) | Both appear on the thank-you screen. The first is the only service promise the product makes and it reads three ways; the second **is still a placeholder in the copy** and would otherwise ship to people who have already converted. **Decided 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c):** contact is promised within one working day, and the thank-you screen carries no telephone line, only the email address. Both thank-you screens in the site file now say so; the email address itself is still a placeholder until the mailbox questions are answered. **(Updated 28 September 2026, Phase 153: they are answered — contact@caprebate.co.uk exists and is read (Word questions 3 and 216, closed 24 September 2026) — but both thank-you screens in the site file still show `[ PLACEHOLDER ]` for the email address.)** |
| **“At account creation, does ‘skip’ mean skip the portal, or a genuinely manual product?”** (Word question 15, “When a client skips making an account, what exactly do they skip?”) | Decides whether DS-W6 is a screen or a manual process. **Decided 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c):** skip means skip the password and the portal, never the email address, and the engagement letter is then sent rather than shown on screen. DS-W6 is a screen; a manual path with no email address is not a website route |
| **“Do we need a compliance role separate from Admin?”** (Word question 44, “Do we need a separate login for data protection work?”) | The compliance registers are ADMIN's today only because no other role exists. A super user is not a data protection officer, and the procedures' two-person check is one login in this build. **Phase 129 defined the role, scoped to the registers, and switched it off**: the answer decides whether it is switched on and whether it reads the audit log. **Decided 15 September 2026 (Phase 132c):** not yet. Compliance stays with Admin, the role stays switched off, and it is revisited when the data protection officer is appointed and has a login |
| **“If money laundering supervision applies to us, what risk-rating scale do we use for a client, and which kinds of claimant must a check cover?”** (Word question 45, “If anti-money-laundering rules apply, how do we rate client risk?”) | Customer due diligence is built and switched off; the position paper names no scale, no threshold and five claimant types, so none is invented |
| **The `step_id` slugs for the funnel's first four screens** (a Measurement Plan amendment, section 5.3, **drafted 23 September 2026 and set out under this table**) | Four of the six assessment screens send no screen name at all, so a funnel report can order them and cannot name them. **It is the data protection officer's review, not a code change** — the plan says so in terms, and the allow-list check enforces it. It also decides what three of the ten events mean in the **served** assessment, which is one page load per screen, and so it **blocks DS-W16** |
| **“Is it right that there is no existing client data to import?”** (Word question 46, “Is it true we have no existing client records to bring in?”) | **The data-import line of the price is nothing**, because there is no real client data anywhere — the seed is invented, and no phase has ever held a real client. A one-line confirmation from Lakhbir. If the answer is no, an import is priced under N2 and the consent history, never as a spreadsheet load |

### The Measurement Plan amendment, drafted 23 September 2026 and waiting on the data protection officer

_Phase 146g. **This is a draft of a document amendment, not a change to any code.** The
Measurement Plan's own rule (section 5.3) is that adding a parameter value is **an amendment to
that document, reviewed by the DPO — not a code change somebody makes on a Tuesday**, and its
open question 3 asks the developer in terms to "confirm the enum of `step_id` values against the
built flow, and add them to this document rather than only to the code". This is that
confirmation, written down so it can be reviewed rather than assumed._

**WHAT IS TRUE TODAY.** The funnel has six screens. **Only two of them send a `step_id`** —
`Q2` sends `property_basics` and `Q3` sends `spend` — because those are the only two the plan's
existing list clearly fits. The other four send **`step_index` alone**, so a funnel report can
order them and cannot name them. `STEP_ID` in "Caprebate site.html" is the map, and it is
deliberately two entries long.

**THE AMENDMENT ASKED FOR — four slugs, for section 5.3's `step_id` row:**

| Screen | What it actually asks | Proposed slug | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Q1` | "Have you already made a capital allowances claim on the fixtures in this building?" | **`prior_claim`** | **New.** Nothing on the existing list describes it. It is the client's own earlier claim |
| `Q1a` | "Do you know whether a previous owner ever claimed on them?" | **`prior_owner_claim`** | **New.** `ownership` is on the list but means who owns it, which is a different question, and a slug that reads as the wrong question is worse than none |
| `Q1b` | "Does the business pay UK income tax or corporation tax?" | **`entity`** | **Already on the list.** It is the business's tax position, which is what `entity` means there |
| `Q1c` | "What brought you here?" | **`trigger`** _(or `timing`, already on the list)_ | **The one to decide.** The screen's own help says "this decides which set of tax rules applies", so it is the *event* — a purchase, a build, a refurbishment — rather than a date. `timing` is on the list and would need no amendment; `trigger` is accurate and would. **The DPO's call, and either is safe: neither is personal data and both are a fixed set** |

**NONE OF THESE IS PERSONAL DATA**, and none is composed from a question label or from anything
a client typed: they are four fixed strings chosen in advance, exactly as section 5.3 requires
of `step_id` ("never composed from a question label, never free text"). **The caution on small
numbers in section 5.3 is unaffected**: these name a screen, not a business.

**NOTHING CHANGES UNTIL THE REVIEW.** The wrapper's lists in "Caprebate site.html" are held to
the plan by `node 03-app/caprebate-analytics-allowlist-check.cjs` (check B2), which fails if the
code's list and the plan's list differ. So the plan is amended first and the code follows —
which is the control working, not an obstacle to it.

**AND IT BLOCKS DS-W16.** The served assessment is one page load per screen, so the same review
should settle what `assessment_step_view`, `assessment_step_back` and `assessment_error` mean in
a server-rendered funnel before any call site is wired into it.

## 5.4 · Two consequences of decisions already taken, which a developer inherits

- **Anyone holding the signing link can sign.** Moving the letter out of the funnel means the signer is no longer the person in the session who answered every question; the link can be forwarded to a colleague or a bookkeeper. **The authority tick and the typed name are the only controls left**, which is why the typed name must be recorded verbatim and compared against the named party. It is a weaker control than a session, it is a consequence of the decision rather than a defect in it, and **the token must be single-purpose, expiring, revocable, scoped to one engagement, never a login, and never reused after a letter is superseded** — a letter edited behind a live link no longer matches its own hash.
- **There is no card processor and there is no PCI scope.** Invoice plus bank transfer, **a gapless invoice number series**, raised, sent and cancelled recorded — and, since CRM 7 of 10, **paid, outstanding and overdue read from the invoice, its credit notes and the money recorded against it** — and **manual reconciliation with an audit trail**. Nothing tells the system a transfer arrived.

## 5.5 · What the CRM needs and this build cannot have — CRM 10 of 10

**The ten CRM phases more than doubled what a developer inherits**, and every item below is either
the CRM's half of something §5.1 already names, or a thing only the CRM needs. Counted on
12 September 2026 by reading the tree and migrating a fresh database. **The middle column was counted
again on 16 September 2026**, after the CRM follow-on, Phase 129, Phase 132 and Phase 132e had added migrations 020 to
029; the other two columns are the CRM's own and did not move. The `src/` and `test/` counts leave out the
`.bak-phase` copies. **Count them again rather than quote them**:

| | Migrations 001–008, before the CRM | Migrations 001–029, today | What the CRM added |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tables | 32 | 97 | 55 |
| Triggers | 6 | 274 | 236 — **most of the refusals live here, at the database** |
| Indexes | 17 | 104 | 76 |
| Migration files | 8 | 29, 7,166 lines | 11, 5,238 lines |
| Source under `src/` | — | 95 files, 43,606 lines | 37 modules, 21,829 lines |
| Routes the code registers | — | 166 | 91 in the ten page modules the CRM added, and the finance routes CRM 7 of 10 widened |
| The back end's `test/` folder | — | 44 files, 21,930 lines | 16 files, 9,643 lines |

**What Phase 132 added to the schema, extended and never rebuilt.** **022** creates `claim_version_stamps` (the case
stamp), `deletion_log` (Retention Procedure 4.3: one entry per deletion, naming a record by kind and id, never by
person; verified once, never deleted) and `deletion_restorations` (a restoration only against a logged deletion).
**023** creates `surveyor_control_observations` (the blank-field control, never changed or deleted) and adds
`surveyor_rate_events.blank_control`. **024** drops and recreates the `claim_facts_key_insert` trigger with migration
015's key list plus `PARTNERSHIP_HAS_COMPANY_MEMBER`; no table, row or other trigger changes.

**What Phase 132e added, on 16 September 2026.** **025** adds `bookings.cancelled_at` and `cancelled_by`, and
`booking_changes.cancelled`, with three triggers: a booking is written uncancelled, a cancellation names both who and
when, and a cancelled call is final. **026** adds the `READ_ONLY` role row, dropping `roles_fixed_insert` for that one
row and recreating it. **027** creates `client_filing_confirmations` (the client's own filing confirmation, party always
CLIENT, append-only). **028** rebuilds `introducer_reward_setting` to allow model C and hold its percentage — the four
triggers naming it dropped first and recreated word for word — and creates `introducer_percentage_entries`, a ledger of
accruals and reversing entries that is never changed or deleted. **029** creates `fee_discounts`, a proposed
multi-site discount recorded and never applied.

**Nothing of the CRM fires anything, and nothing of it is hosted.** Every seam it uses already
existed — `EmailTransport`, `SignatureProvider`, the register stub, `TextExtractor`, `ObjectStore`
— and **both register gates stay shut** (disposition row 1.8).

1. **UK data residency for every new store.** The CRM's fifty-five tables hold client, staff and
   introducer data, the identity store and the compliance registers among them, in the same
   SQLite file as everything else; its documents go through the same `ObjectStore` port; its
   letters and notices queue in the same outbox. **In production every one of those — the
   database, the document store, their backups, the logs and the email provider's message store —
   is hosted in the United Kingdom.** Retention periods are not a developer's recommendation: they
   come from the Retention Determinations, and the retention list shows UNSET as unset (rows 18.8,
   29.10). **Any host, email provider, calendar service or accounting package is a processor, named
   in clause 7 of the engagement letter and section 4 of the privacy notice before it carries client
   data** (row B4.11). Work item DS-W11.

2. **Email deliverability before any template leaves the outbox, and the invoice email only after
   the fraud procedure is in force.** The CRM queues more than the first build did — templates from
   the claim page, the automation's notices to an owner, the stage notices to an introducing firm,
   the compliance registers' draft letters — and **every row is written with `sent_at` NULL and
   labelled not sent**. Implementing `EmailTransport` is a swap; deliverability is not: SPF, DKIM
   and DMARC on the sending domain, bounce and complaint handling, and a suppression list — and a
   real transport **must keep calling `isDoNotContact`**, and keep the template store's refusals of
   bank details, invoices and tipping-off words. Where the mailboxes live was *“Are the business
   mailboxes going on IONOS, where the mail already points, or on Microsoft 365, which the runbook
   assumes?”* **(Updated 28 September 2026, Phase 153: answered — the mailboxes are on IONOS.
   contact@, data.protection@ and security@caprebate.co.uk exist there (Word question 3, closed 24 September
   2026), and Sadique Maskeen, the data protection officer, reads all three (Word question 216, closed
   24 September 2026).)** **The invoice email is not built.** `INVOICE_FRAUD_PROCEDURE` in
   `src/web/automation.ts` says the procedure is a draft addition to SOP 9, not in force, and a test
   fails if the code says otherwise; build it only once the solicitor, the data protection officer
   and the developer have signed that procedure off. **Inbox synchronisation is not in the ten
   phases** (row 25.2): the CRM is outbound, through the outbox. Work item DS-W5.

3. **Calendar synchronisation. Booking inside the application is built; external sync is not.**
   Built: `src/web/schedule.ts`, `bookings` and `booking_changes`, each person's working times in UK
   time, staff scheduling from the calendar, and the client moving their own call — with `isBookable`
   asked again **at the write** every time. Not built: no iCalendar object is generated (stub 1), and
   nothing reaches Outlook or Google. **The application stays the source of truth for a client's
   call**: publish its bookings to staff calendars first, and if a two-way link is ever wanted, an
   external change must go through `isBookable` like any other — never write a booking the
   application did not check (rows 19.6, 25.3). Work item DS-W12.

4. **Accounting-software integration as an export seam, not a build.** Built: invoices on the claim
   with one gapless number series, the amounts unwritable, credit notes and money received as rows,
   overdue read rather than typed, and an audited CSV export of the lists (`list.export`). **The
   seam is one more export of the same kind** — invoices, credit notes and money received, in the
   chosen package's own import format, read-only, for Accounting and Admin, audited with its columns
   and row count like the list export. **Nothing is pushed into the package, and nothing tells the
   system a transfer arrived**; a person who has seen the bank records it. Which package is chosen
   changes the file's columns and nothing else, so **nothing waits on the choice** (rows 13.13, 25.1,
   29.7). Work item DS-W13.

5. **Data import: there is no real client data anywhere, so the answer is nothing to import.** The
   seed is invented, every postcode in it is from the fictional ZZ range, and no phase has ever held a
   real client, lead, introducer or document. **The line in the price for importing data is
   nothing.** That is put to Lakhbir as a one-line confirmation — *“Is it right that there is no
   existing client data to import?”* — because if a spreadsheet or an inbox of real enquiries does
   exist, importing it is not a load: every person in it is a contact or an identity-store record
   under N2, with a consent history and a lawful basis, and nothing in it may become a guess under N4
   (row 29.9).

6. **The WordPress or Node question, now with the CRM's size in the price.** *“Does WordPress
   replace the rest of the system, or sit beside it?”* is still unconfirmed, and **the CRM makes the
   two answers further apart, not closer.** If the host can run a Node process, the CRM costs almost
   nothing extra to ship: it is the same process behind the same reverse proxy, and the work is the
   production store — SQLite is the prototype's, and the lists are capped and authorised row by row
   (rows 21.3, 27.10). **If it cannot, a PHP rebuild now has to re-home the table above** — and the
   expensive part is not the screens. It is the 236 triggers through which the database itself
   refuses a skipped gate, a queued notice without the client's yes, a second live invoice, a
   received request nobody uploaded, a deleted record — each with a test that must be ported and
   seen to fail. Quote both routes, and get the one-sentence confirmation first.

7. **Penetration testing of every new route.** Not performed — for the first build or for the CRM
   (§5.1 item 4). The payload proof and the refusal tests are not a penetration test and are not
   offered as one. **Test every route in §3.1 against a seeded copy, never real data**, and give
   these the most time: every `{id}` swapped for another organisation's (the refusal and *not found*
   must be indistinguishable); every `view.json` and both CSV exports, as each role; global search
   and the identity store's own search; the compliance registers and the merge; the upload `PUT`;
   the settings screen's refused keys; and, **before it is ever switched on**, the public referral
   form at `/refer`, which will be the first unauthenticated write the CRM exposes. Work item DS-W15.

**Not on this list, on purpose.** **Marketing automation** (row 25.11) is not built in the ten
phases; if it ever is, it writes only to a contact whose `marketing_optin` is set for that channel,
never to a DO_NOT_CONTACT organisation, never from the identity store, and through the outbox.
**Infrastructure configuration and a deployment process** (rows 21.8, 26.7, 26.8, 26.13): nothing is
deployed anywhere, so there is none to hand over and none is invented — **the tree is the
handover**, and staging, production, backups and recovery are work item DS-W14, behind the
backup-exclusion question (rows 18.7, 21.5, 26.10).
